| 152 | **The two United States statutes that bear directly on what this text claims — the termination right and the moral-rights waiver formalities — appeared nowhere in the dossier.** Searching every file for `203`, `106A` and `VARA` on 2026-09-19 returned **nothing**. **17 U.S.C. §203(a)** subjects *"the exclusive or nonexclusive **grant of a transfer or license** of copyright … executed by the author"* to termination, and §203(a)(5) says termination may be effected *"**notwithstanding any agreement to the contrary**"*. **Section 4 is a licence, so the word *irrevocable* in §2.2 and §4.1 does not stop a United States author or their heirs terminating it after thirty-five years.** **17 U.S.C. §106A(e)(1)** allows a moral-rights waiver only *"in a written instrument **signed by the author**"* that *"**specifically identif[ies] the work, and uses of that work**"* — **a LICENSE file in a repository is neither signed nor use-specific**, so §12.1 is unlikely to operate as a waiver there | **[EXTERNAL]** **Recorded with both directions, and no change to the text.** **Unfavourable**: the flat *irrevocable* is, as to Section 4 in the United States, contradicted by statute; what saves the document from being wrong is §15.4 (§15.2 in 1.1 and the 1.2 draft), which reforms or severs per jurisdiction — so the clause **shrinks there** rather than the document being false. **Favourable, and this is where the three footings stop looking redundant**: §203 reaches a *"transfer or license"*, and **Section 3 is a surrender, which is neither**; §203(b) returns *"rights … covered by the terminated grants"*, and **Section 5 is a covenant, not a grant**. **Neither limb is safe**: abandonment of copyright in the United States is judge-made and unsettled (the question §5 of `ACD-1.0.jurisdictions.md` opens with), and a covenant could itself be read as an *"agreement to the contrary"*. **Two uncertainties in series, not a defence.** **On VARA the scope must not be inflated**: it covers *works of visual art* only, and audiovisual works are excluded by definition — **software is not within it at all**, so for this instrument's usual subject matter it barely arises. **§12.1 failing there is the designed path into §12.2**, whose own enforceability is a question for counsel (B1). Worked in `ACD-1.0.jurisdictions.md` §5a and §5b **潰し方**: **米国の制定法は動かせない。** 我々にできるのは **(a) *irrevocable* が §4 について米国法上は制定法に反することを、我々の側から述べること**、**(b) 文書を救っているのが §5 の covenant であると示すこと**（終了権が行使されても不行使の約束は残る）、**(c) 人格権の waiver の方式要件を `jurisdictions.md` に条文で置くこと**。(a)(c) は実行済み。 |
| 153 | **The jurisdiction map argued from summaries of four foreign statutes rather than from the statutes.** Until 2026-09-19 the United Kingdom section described s.9(3) as *"a provision to the effect that the author is the person who made the arrangements"* without quoting it, said nothing at all about **s.29A** (the text-and-data-mining exception) or **s.87** (moral-rights waiver), and the Japan section cited **59 条** without quoting **30 条の 4** or **60 条**. **A reviewer checking a jurisdiction claim goes to the statute, and ours could not be checked against one.** | **[EXTERNAL]** **Fixed by fetching all four and quoting them, and two of the four moved the argument against us.** **Japan**: 30 条の 4 is **not limited to non-commercial use**, so Section 6 buys less there than in the EU or the UK — **stated in those words**. What it does buy is narrower and now said narrowly: the exception carries a proviso (*不当に害する*) whose application is contested, and **an express permission removes the need to argue it**. **United Kingdom**: s.29A(1) is limited to *"research for a **non-commercial** purpose"* **and requires a *"sufficient acknowledgement"***, so there ACD gives **more than the statute and with fewer conditions** (§6.1, §10.2). **The strongest thing the exercise produced is an argument for §9 that needs no theory**: s.178 defines *computer-generated* as a work with *"no human author"* and **s.9(3) then supplies one**, while the United States Copyright Office holds that purely AI-generated material has no human authorship at all — **the same material has a rights-holder in one country and none in the other, which is precisely what §9.3 relieves the user of having to determine**. **And on moral rights the United Kingdom lands better than the United States**: s.87(2) requires a signed instrument for a *waiver*, but **s.87(1) recognises *consent* with no formality**, which is the shape §12.2 already has. **None of this is advice** — every reading here is ours (B1) **潰し方**: **制定法は動かせないが、要約ではなく条文で論じることはできる。** 4 本とも取得して引用済みで、**うち 2 本は我々に不利な向きへ議論を動かした**（日本の 30 条の 4 は非営利に限られない等）。**残る作業は、新しい法域を足すときに同じ規律（要約でなく条文）を守ること。** |
| 154 | **The clause-mapping table written this morning to prevent exactly this class of error said §1 needed no remapping, and §1 is where the numbers moved.** `ACD-1.2-CHANGELIST.md` carried a row reading *"§1.1〜§14.x → the same numbers; the §1 reorder (E13) did not move numbers"*. **It did move them.** Matching the definitions by their defined term rather than by number: **1.0 §1.2 *Work* → 1.2 §1.3; §1.3 *Dedicator* → §1.4; §1.4 *You* → §1.6; §1.6 *Moral Rights* → §1.2.** **So a submission-facing citation to §1.4 — written to mean *You* — points at *Dedicator* in 1.2, and Check 472 passes it because the number exists.** **The cause is a measurement that answered a different question than the one that mattered**: the morning's check compared the *set of clause numbers* in §1–§14 and found it identical, which it is — the *contents* moved beneath it | **[CLOSED]** **Fixed, and a tool built rather than a rule written.** The table now carries the definition map, derived **by defined term, not by number**, so the direction of the reorder is never guessed. `measure_citation_drift.py` reports, for the four submission-facing documents, which cited clauses vanish, which keep the number while the text changes, and which are effectively the same. **Measured on the day: of 59 cited clauses, 3 vanish (Check 472 catches those), 18 keep the number with changed text, and 7 of those 18 fall below 0.15 similarity — a different clause wearing the same number.** **It is a tool and not a Check on purpose**: the threshold for *"changed materially"* is a judgement, and enforcing one would turn legitimate repairs (E4's rewording of §6.4, say) red. **The general shape is one this dossier keeps meeting**: a check of *existence* cannot see a change of *meaning*, and the thing that reassured us — "the numbers are identical" — was true |
| 155 | **The text frozen for discussion on 2026-09-17 carries a ninety-column line that one of that day's own repairs put there, and the freeze makes it permanent.** Measuring the wrapped width of the three texts: **ACD-1.0 has seven lines over seventy-nine columns (longest eighty)**, **ACD-1.1 has eight (longest ninety)**, and the ninety-column line is in §4.6 — the sentence errata **E20** rewrote that morning. **A licence is attached to a submission as plain text and read in a mail client**; ragged wrapping is the kind of thing a reviewer notices before reading a word of it. **`FROZEN.md` pins the file by SHA-256, so it cannot be repaired in 1.1** | **[CLOSED]** **Recorded, bounded, and fixed where it can be fixed.** **ACD-1.2 is now reflowed to seventy-nine columns with zero exceptions**, and the reflow was done under an assertion that the **normalised word stream is byte-identical before and after** — the text did not change, only where the lines break. **Two things temper this.** The discipline was never perfectly held: **1.0 already carried seven over-length lines before we touched anything**, so 1.1 made an existing lapse worse rather than introducing one. And **the version that will actually be submitted is 1.2**, which is clean, so the permanent defect sits in the text put forward for *discussion* rather than for approval. **What it changes**: the reflow check now runs on the draft before a freeze, not after. **The general shape**: a freeze makes every property of the file permanent, including the ones nobody measured — and the measurement here took one command **検証 2026-09-23**: **条文側は例外ゼロである。** 素朴に `length>79` で数えると **1 行**出るが、**それはヘッダと本文を分ける 80 本のダッシュのセパレータ**（141 行目）であって条文ではない。**この区別を書いておかないと、同じ再測定が「主張が偽だ」と繰り返し報告する** ——本日それを実際にやりかけた。1.0（7 行）/ 1.1（10 行）は凍結のまま。 |
| 156 | **Section 3.2 names a category of right that European Union law says cannot be waived, and the dossier had never cited the provision that says so.** §3.2 states that the surrender *"includes any right to **compensation, remuneration, levy, or royalty** arising from any use of the Work"*, and §4.2 licenses *"lend, rent"* while §1.2 (§1.3 in 1.1 and the 1.2 draft) brings **audiovisual material** inside the defined Work. **Directive 2006/115/EC Article 5 is headed *"Unwaivable right to equitable remuneration"* and says in terms: *"The right to obtain an equitable remuneration for rental **cannot be waived** by authors or performers."*** Article 6 lets Member States limit the lending right only *"provided that at least authors obtain a remuneration for such lending"*. **Read alone, §3.2 purports to give away something a directive declares ungiveable** | **[CLOSED]** **Recorded with the provision quoted, and the text left alone — for a reason this sweep had already written down.** **The user's position does not change**, on three grounds: §3.2 describes the scope of §3.1, which carries *"to the fullest extent permitted by the law of each jurisdiction"*, so in such a jurisdiction the clause **shrinks rather than lies**; equitable remuneration is typically collected from producers, importers or lending institutions through collecting societies, so **an unwaivable claim surviving creates no obligation on You**; and Article 5(1) is triggered by a **transfer of the rental right to a phonogram or film producer**, which a dedication to the world is not. **The text is not changed because §3.2 opens *"The surrender in Section 3.1 is made …"*** — it is anchored to the qualified clause, and the rule this sweep set for itself (in `ACD-1.1-SELF-AUDIT.md` §3g) is that an anchored clause is clean. **What changed is that we cite the article before a reviewer does.** **Limits stated**: national implementations go further in places (German copyright law gives remuneration claims for rental and public lending), and **private-copying levies come from a different directive we have not read at source, so this entry does not mention them**. Worked in `ACD-1.0.jurisdictions.md` §6a |
| 157 | **The list has a name for the category this instrument falls into, it has been in use on both lists since at least 2019, and we had never looked it up.** Three reports supplied from outside used the term *crayon license*; **searching our own archive corpus found it in fifteen files**. Bruce Perens defines it (`license-discuss`, 2025-10-01): *"A 'crayon license' is a license obviously produced by a **non-legal-professional**, which has obvious faults that a legal professional would know to avoid."* **We meet the first half of that definition.** | **[EXTERNAL]** **Recorded, and it changed how B1 is modelled.** The most consequential sentence is not the definition but the **Licensing Committee chair's**, written during the CAL review (`license-review`, 2019-06-27): *"**The high likelihood that the license would be interpreted in significantly different ways in different legal jurisdictions militates against its approval.** Although the CAL is not, by any means, a 'crayon' license, **it has the potential for the same negative consequence, which is unpredictable interpretation**."* **The harm named is not that a non-lawyer wrote it. It is that the reading varies by jurisdiction — and the label is a proxy for that harm, not the harm itself.** **This splits B1 in two**: the *fact* of legal review stays immovable, but **interpretive variance is ours to reduce — and errata E19, E20, E21, E23 and E24 were all doing exactly that without a name for it.** **The label is also not fatal**: CAL was approved after that comment; the **approved** Unlicense is called crayon on the list (2024-04-05); Perens names the **approved** SIL Open Font License as a deprecation candidate (2022-12-13). **And the converse holds against us**: ACD is structurally on the side of the harm — §3, §4 and §5 are placed side by side precisely because they operate differently in different jurisdictions, and §3.3, §12.2 and §15.2 are per-jurisdiction branches. **Taking that on as design is not an answer to the chair's concern.** Worked in `ACD-1.0.review-labels.md` §1.98 **潰し方**: **委員長の基準（法域で解釈が大きく異なる見込みは承認に不利）は我々が変えられない。** 我々にできるのは **(a) 分岐する箇所を数えて名指しすること**（#162 の 29 箇所）、**(b) 分岐を*閉じる*第 2 の足を各所に置くこと**（§4.4 / §8.6 / §9.3 / §12.2）、**(c) E25 のように、2 つの canon が反対向きに働く状態を作らないこと**。 |
| 158 | **Two of the three reports supplied on 2026-09-19 misidentify the object they analyse, and one of them does so in its opening sentence.** The adversarial report describes the licence as 「現在 `LICENSES/ACD-1.0.against.md` として配置されている文書」（**原文は日本語**・以下同じ） — **`against.md` is the list of adverse facts, not the licence**, which is `ACD-1.0.txt`. The process report states the repository has 「91コミット以上が積み重なっており」 (it has an order of magnitude more) and infers the contents of `against.md` from its filename (「想定される反論や課題をまとめたと推測される」). The third report proposes obtaining a 「英訳」を証明つきで用意せよという趣旨の提案 on the premise that the original may be Japanese; **the licence has been English-only from the first version, and §15.8 (§15.4 in 1.1 and the 1.2 draft) says so in terms** | **[CLOSED]** **Recorded because the same discipline applies in both directions.** `feedback_source_vs_content` says provenance bears on presentation and weighting, never on correctness — **so a premise error does not discredit the findings, and the findings do not excuse the premise error.** **The reports were used, and used hard**: one carried the term *crayon license*, which turned out to be in heavy use on both lists and led to the Licensing Committee chair's sentence about **unpredictable interpretation** (#157), the single most consequential reviewer statement found this month; another named *contra proferentem*, which sent us to 1.0 §15.3 and its removal in 1.1. **What the errors change is the weighting**: a report that has not opened `ACD-1.0.txt` cannot be relied on for claims *about the text*, and two of its central claims — that a sixty-day review decides the outcome, and that a licence without legal review is a 「致命的な欠陥とみなされる」 at submission — **are contradicted by our own measurements** (median 101 days to decision; the Licensing Committee chair calling legal review *"recommended"* and *"not a blocker"*). **Their value was the vocabulary they carried, not the conclusions they drew.** Verbatim in `rounds/`. **⚠ Corrected 2026-09-20**: this entry first rendered those five phrases in English inside the `*"…"*` convention, **which in this dossier means a verbatim quotation a reviewer can find in the source**. **The reports are written in Japanese**, so what stood there were *our translations wearing the marks of quotation* — searchable by nobody. They now carry the Japanese, and the English appears only as description. **Found by the quotation sweep on the day after the entry was written** (#165), and it is the second class of fidelity error that sweep caught in our own fresh work |
| 159 | **A drafting change was reasoned from an error that had been sitting in our own prepared answer for two weeks, and a later change in the same version line then made the error load-bearing without anyone re-testing it.** 1.0 §15.3 disapplies **two** canons: contra proferentem (ambiguity against the drafter) and the narrow construction of grants (against the grantee). **In a dedication they run in opposite directions** — resolving ambiguity against the drafter is resolving it *for* the recipient. **`review-responses-boilerplate.md` Q23, written 2026-08-26, treated them as one** (*"the recipient does not benefit from a narrower grant"*); **`ACD-1.1-CHANGELIST.md` §2.5, written 2026-09-09, deleted the clause on the same reading** (*"§15.1 が既に … 同じ方向を二重に言っている"*); **and errata E20, written 2026-09-17, limited §15.1 to *"in respect of the Work"* without going back to the deletion it had just invalidated.** The result in the 1.2 draft: **§15.1 reaches none of §16, and the clause that had kept both canons off §16 was gone**, so two canons ran unordered over §16.4 — one reading its restriction narrowly, the other its permissions narrowly. **The chronology is verifiable** (`git log -S` on each string) but **does not establish that the Q&A caused the deletion**; what it establishes is that the same conflation was in both, earliest in the answer | **[CLOSED]** **Recorded, and repaired in 1.2 rather than only noted** (§15.6 restores 1.0 §15.3 word for word; errata E25; Q23 rewritten with the correction named rather than swapped in). **Why it was repaired and not merely recorded**: #157 is the Licensing Committee chair saying that a high likelihood of **significantly different interpretations in different jurisdictions** militates against approval — and **two canons pulling opposite ways on one clause with nothing to order them is that harm, manufactured by us**. **⚠ Three things this does not say.** (1) **Nothing about the Work changed in either direction** — §16 is not a term of the Work and binds no recipient of it (§10.5, §16.6), so the whole episode lives in the clause that keeps the identifier pointing at one fixed text. (2) **The repair costs 28 machinery words** against B3, and B3's own reduction table had scored those 28 words as the easiest deletion in the text — **so the same measurement that said "cut this" is the measurement we are now overriding, and it has not been withdrawn**. (3) **A clause that disapplies contra proferentem still reads as a drafter fending off a rule meant to protect the other side**, and that reading does not disappear because our reason is different (Q23). **The general form is the one worth carrying**: *the analysis layer feeds the text layer, so an error in a prepared answer can be enacted*, and *two edits landing in one version are not tested against each other unless someone goes back*. **It was found because an outside report used the words "contra proferentem"** — the vocabulary, not the conclusions (#158) **潰し方は実行済み** —— §15.6 が 1.0 §15.3 を一字一句戻した（errata E25）。**2 つの canon が 1 条の上で反対方向に働き、順序を述べる語が本文に無い**状態は解消している。 |
| 160 | **Of the four things the list has proposed that a reusable licence must not do, ACD-1.0 clears all four, and its successor clears three and trades on the fourth.** *(Corrected 2026-09-24: this entry had said ACD clears three, attributing 1.1's Steward exception to 1.0.)* Marc Jones (`license-discuss`, 2019-02-11) argued that *"the requirement that a license be **reusable** would significantly reduce the risk of a return to vanity license proliferation"* and named what to exclude: a licence that hard-codes a specific person or company as licensor, one that *"hard code[s] the licensor being in an **unreasonably privileged position**"*, one that requires *"significant changes or modifications to the license text to be used by others"*, and one only its sponsor can comply with. **Three are clean and measured** (§16.3 plus zero proper nouns and no substitution point in the clauses — the one blank, in §16.1's notice template, is filled in the adopter's own notice; no edit required to adopt; §10.1 imposes no condition so no one can fail to comply). **In ACD-1.0 the second is clear too**: its §16.4 closes the name and the one identifier `ACD-1.0` to every distributor alike and names no exception — at the cost of errata E14. **In ACD-1.1 and the 1.2 draft it is not**: to close E14 their §16.4 protects the whole `ACD-N` / `ACD-N.N` family *"except by the Steward"*, and their §16.5 defines that role as the party who first published the text under that name — **a privilege held by one party and written into the text itself** | **[TRADE]** **Recorded as a trade, not as a defect, and the trade is stated rather than defended.** **§16.6 puts §16.4 and §16.5 outside the terms of the Work** — they bind no recipient of it — so nothing about what anyone may do with a work is affected; **but Jones's criterion is about the text, and carries no such limit**. **⚠ The converse, at the same weight**: stewardship itself is ordinary — Apache, MPL and the GPL all have one. **What is unusual is writing it into the licence rather than operating it outside**; MIT, 0BSD and the Unlicense name no steward in their texts. **The reason it is written is errata E14**: an identifier has to keep denoting one fixed text, and until a registry does that job the text is the only place it can be done (`ACD-1.1-CHANGELIST.md` §2.6 calls §16.4 *"the bridge until registration"*). **So the honest position is that family-wide identifier stability and the absence of in-text privilege cannot both be had: 1.0 has the second and pays with E14, and the successor chose the first.** A reviewer may reasonably prefer the second; **what we must not do is answer the point by quoting §16.6 as though the criterion had a limit it does not have.** **⚠ And the criterion is a proposal, not a rule** — no evidence was found that the list adopted it. Worked in `review-labels.md` §1.99 **entry 自身が「取引であって欠陥ではない」と述べている。** §16.4 / §16.5 の steward 特権は **E14**（識別子が 1 つの固定テキストを指し続けるための橋）が理由で、**#210 が外部の証人（GPL 命名の *debacle*）と反対側（OSI の実務は 1 対多）を両方持っている。** **合否は、厳しい側を選んだと自分から述べているか。** |
| 161 | **Two senior participants, seven years apart and in unrelated contexts, have said that *why the submitter is submitting* is a proper input to the review — and our answer to that question is "a portfolio experiment in AI self-direction".** **Richard Fontana, then writing from an `opensource.org` address as a director** (`license-discuss`, 2019-02-06): *"I think that the **business model of the license submitter can be a material consideration** when assessing whether the proposed license meets the OSD"*, adding that licences submitted by businesses *"tend not be used by other licensors (apart from forks)"* and that *"that was one of the reasons why the vanity corporate licenses of the earlier era of open source were problematic"*. **OSI Moderators** (both lists, 2026-09-09): the lists are *"being used as a **proof-of-concept** to demonstrate that an AI system can autonomously engage and even draft an open source license for review"* (B14). **Separately, Fontana (2020-01-04) states B2 as a policy preference**: the OSI *"may have taken the wrong path very early on by developing a process that **encouraged submission of novel, community-untested licenses**"* and *"it would be better for OSI to have the expectation that **license review will only take place some months or years after a license is already in practical use**"* — **the same coupling of no-adoption to no-review that Lukas Atkinson stated in one sentence (#113)**. **A third strand attaches to the name**: Bradley M. Kuhn (`license-discuss`, 2022-12-13) called a licence vanity-style because its name was *"either designed to promote the name of the org, or **it's designed to make it seem like it's the only appropriate license for work on the general topic**"* — the first limb misses us, **the second can land on "Autonomous"** | **[CLOSED]** **Recorded, and not to be argued with on the list.** **The purpose is a fact about us, and stating it first is the only move available** — `submission.md` §E already discloses the drafting arrangement, and the moderator's five questions were answered on the day they arrived (2026-09-07). **⚠ What this does not establish**: none of these messages is about ACD; Fontana's 2019 remark was about business submitters and is quoted here because the reasoning transfers, not because it was aimed at us; **and a stated preference by one participant is not the review standard** — the published criteria say nothing about the submitter's motive. **⚠ The converse that actually matters**: **an instrument whose author has no stake in it being adopted is the opposite of a vanity licence** — §10.1 imposes no condition, §16.3 lets anyone apply it with no relationship to us, and there is no field, project or party it privileges in respect of the Work. **The suspicion is about why it exists; the text is the answer to what it does, and the two are not the same question.** **The naming limb is answerable by §16.3** (*"not specific to any project, person, organisation, jurisdiction, or field of endeavour"*) **but the objection arrives before the text is opened**, and the same thread shows a name change dissolving it. Worked in `review-labels.md` §1.99 |
| 162 | **On the second readability measure we have ever applied to ourselves, we are again the heaviest instrument in the comparison set — and this one sits on the exact axis the Licensing Committee chair named.** Counting the phrases by which a text makes its own operation depend on local law (`jurisdiction`, `governing law`, `permitted by law`, `to the extent permitted`, `held invalid|unenforceable`, `applicable law`, `where … law`) over the SPDX verbatim texts: **ACD-1.2's operative part carries 29 in 4,805 words = 6.04 per thousand**, against Unlicense 5.08 (on 197 words), OSL-3.0 4.28, CAL-1.0 3.12, EPL-2.0 2.82, MPL-2.0 2.05, Apache-2.0 1.90, and **MIT, MIT-0, 0BSD and BSD-3-Clause at 0.00**. **Twenty of eighty clauses (25%) operate differently according to the law of the jurisdiction.** **#157 records the chair writing that *"the high likelihood that the license would be interpreted in significantly different ways in different legal jurisdictions militates against its approval"*** — so this is not a general readability complaint, it is a count of the thing that was named | **[EXTERNAL]** **Recorded, and the count is not the verdict — the classification is.** **MIT's zero is silence, not clarity**: MIT does not say what happens when one of its terms is unenforceable where you are, it simply does not address it. **Our 29 are 29 places where the text says what happens**, so the same number reads as *more variance* or as *variance named and closed*, and the number alone cannot choose. **What decides it is the split, which `ACD-1.1-SELF-AUDIT.md` §3l sets out clause by clause**: five branch-clauses are closed and say so (§4.4, §9.3), **three were closed but did not say so until errata E26 repaired the asymmetry on the day of this measurement**, two move only in the recipient's favour, eight are structural, and **one family of three — the successor-binding limit in §2.8, §5.3 and §12.4 — is open and cannot be closed by drafting**, because it turns on whom an undertaking reaches rather than on what form it takes. **⚠ That last one is the honest residue**: where successors can be bound broadly a work stays safe after the rights change hands, and where they cannot, the Dedicator's heirs may still exercise Moral Rights. **§13.2 is in capitals about exactly this class.** **⚠ And the converse on the residue**: MIT and 0BSD do not have zero of this problem, they have **no answer to the question** — and where §3 is effective there is no surviving right to inherit, so the residue bites only where §3 fails *and* successors cannot be bound. **We have not measured how large that intersection is.** **⚠ What the whole sweep does not measure**: **ambiguity inside a single sentence** — the other half of what the chair's phrase covers — **is not touched by this method at all**, and 20 is a floor derived from literal indicators, not a census (#87) **潰し方**: **数は verdict ではなく分類が verdict であり、分類するのは審査者である。** 我々にできるのは「29 箇所は*変数が名指しされて閉じられている*箇所であって、MIT の 0 は沈黙である」と述べ切ることで、それは実行済み。 |
| 163 | **The half of the chair's objection that matters most is the half we cannot measure, and the reason we cannot is B1 itself.** #157 records the Licensing Committee chair objecting to a *"high likelihood that the license would be interpreted in significantly different ways in different legal jurisdictions"*. **#162 measured one half of that — clauses whose operation branches on local law — and found it countable.** **The other half is ambiguity inside a single sentence, and three mechanical detectors built for it all failed**: a trailing-qualifier detector returned 31 hits of which **2** were real, a negation-over-disjunction detector returned 32 of which **0** were real, and a pronoun-antecedent detector returned 26 of which **1** was real — **89 hits, 3 findings, precision about 3%**. **The reason is not that the detectors were badly written.** Whether a qualifier attaches to one limb or to all of them is not decided by grammar; the third adds one. **⚠ The converse, at the same weight, and it cuts both ways.** **Not being able to measure it is not evidence that there is much of it** — the count is unknown in both directions, and this entry must not be read as conceding that the text is ambiguous. **And the hand sweep is not weak because it is manual, it is weak because it is not repeatable**: a different reader would very likely find a different three. **That is the same observation as `review-precedents.md` §1.52** — the reviewers on this list are pro bono lawyers, and **submitting is itself the route by which a sole drafter gets a lawyer's eye over the text**; | 164 | **The check that watches for collisions in the shared section numbering was not watching three of the eight files that carry it, and one of the three went out of scope on the day this entry was written — by our own hand.** Check 471 face (f) guards a numbering series (`§1.xx`) that is deliberately spread across several dossier files, on the ground that **a reference to a duplicated number resolves and lands on the wrong section**. Its file list was hardcoded to five names. Measured: `dig-2026-09.md` (4 headings) and `objection-map.md` (1) were **never in scope**, and `review-labels.md` (2) left scope the moment it was split out of `reviewer-positions.md` earlier today. **Deriving the set from `LICENSES/ACD-1.0.*.md` instead immediately produced a finding the hardcoded list could not see**: `§1.91` appeared to exist in two files at once | **[CLOSED]** **Fixed by derivation, and the finding it produced turned out to be a second instance of a pattern we had just created ourselves.** `§1.91`'s apparent duplicate was a **pointer heading** left by a split on 2026-09-17 (*"§1.91 and §1.93 moved to …"*), written in a shape that reads to any parser as *owning* that number. **Today's split produced exactly the same shape** (*"§1.98 / §1.99 are in another file"*) — **so the pattern has now occurred twice, two days apart, and the check that would have caught the first one could not see the file.** Both headings were rewritten to carry `§` before the number, which removes the false claim of ownership while keeping the pointer readable. **⚠ What this does not mean**: **no reference was ever wrong because of `§1.91`** — both files were reachable and the pointer said where to go, so the defect was in what a machine would conclude, not in what a reader would find. **⚠ And the converse on derivation**: a derived set can over-reach as easily as a hardcoded one under-reaches. This one is bounded by a single stated ground — **the shared series lives in `ACD-1.0.*.md`** — which is why `REVISION-PROTOCOL.md` §1.5, a document's own section numbering rather than part of the series, falls outside without needing an exception list. **The general form is the one to carry**: **when you split a file, the pointer you leave behind is itself a new object that the checks will read** — and a hardcoded scan list makes the new file invisible at the same moment (the scope-drift class of Checks 124, 411 and 435b) |

The cause is a one-character-class slip: the block had already been whitespace-normalised, so it began `* A new …`, and slicing off `len('    * ')` = six characters removed `* A ne` instead of the indent. **Nothing caught it.** Check 468 measures the draft's word count, clause count, ASCII purity and cross-reference resolution — **all of them over the text after the separator rule**. **The editorial header, which is the only surface that explains to a reader what this draft is, was read by no check at any point.** The owner then sent that file to two external reviewers, so **the reviews were performed on a document with visible damage in its second bullet block** | **[CLOSED]** **Fixed, and closed with Check 468f**: header bullets must begin with a capital letter or a digit. **The rule is deliberately typographic, not semantic** — a check that tried to judge whether a bullet *says* the right thing would be the brittle gate §2.9 refuses. Non-vacuity was measured by restoring the corruption (RED) and removing it again (green). **⚠ The uncomfortable part is not the typo.** It is that **the same session had just written two entries about editing damaging fidelity** (#165, #166) and then damaged a file by editing it. **⚠ And the converse, stated plainly**: the header is *not* the licence. The operative text was never touched, the word count never moved, and **no reader of ACD-1.2's terms was ever affected**. **What was affected is the thing this repository trades on** — that a file which explains itself can be trusted to explain itself. **The general form**: **a check that measures the important part exactly can leave the explaining part entirely unread**, and the explaining part is what reaches a reader first |
| 168 | **Two external reviews of ACD-1.2 came back with zero findings, and the strongest thing they establish is about reviews rather than about the text.** Both are favourable throughout — one is titled a *favourable verification*, the other assigns "appropriate" clause by clause — and **between them they record not one defect, not one ambiguity, and not one open question**. **On the same document, in the same twenty-four hours, we recorded six misquotations (#166), three errata closed (E25–E27), and a corrupted header (#167).** **The header corruption is the decisive test**: it sits in the second bullet block of the file they read, in plain sight, and **neither review mentions it** | **[CLOSED]** **Recorded, and used for exactly what it can carry.** **A review that finds nothing is evidence about the reviewer's reading, not about the reviewed text** — the same discipline `feedback_source_vs_content` applies to provenance. **⚠ This is not a claim that the reviews are worthless.** Two things in them are usable: the Japanese exception's *entry* condition — 著作権法 30 条の 4 requires a non-enjoyment purpose, which can fail where an enjoyment purpose coexists, quite apart from the proviso we had already recorded — is a limb our jurisdictions note did not state (now added, `jurisdictions.md` §2a); | 169 | **The instrument's central structural claim has now been misread three times, and the third reader was outside this project and was trying to be favourable.** §3 surrenders, §4 licenses, §5 covenants, and the design is that they stand **side by side, not in a queue** — the preamble says so, §2.4 says so, and §4.4 says the licence *"is granted independently of Section 3"*. **#24 records that this dossier itself described §4 as a fallback until 2026-08-28; #35 records the same error surviving in two further places** — so with the two outside readings below, **the same misreading has now been made four times by four different readers.** **On 2026-09-20 an external review summarised §4.1–§4.4 as a *"public licence fallback"* for jurisdictions where §3 fails — and marked the clause appropriate in the same table.** **A third external reading, the adversarial one delivered later the same day, does it again**: it describes §4.1–§4.5 as operating *"as a fallback where the surrender in Section 3 is invalid"*. Only the favourable review read it correctly (*"not as an alternative fallback, but side by side and simultaneously"*), so **the observed rate among outside readers is two in three**, on a document whose preamble spends a paragraph denying exactly that reading | **[CLOSED]** **Repaired in 1.2 (errata E28) rather than only recorded, because this is the first *observed* instance of the harm #157 names.** Until now "a reader who stops at one clause is misled" was our hypothesis, argued from E19–E21; **here there are readers, and they were misled in the same direction three times.** The old wording denied the fallback frame by naming it (*"does not depend on Section 3 being ineffective"*); the new wording states the positive (*"not as a fallback for it: it takes effect whether or not Section 3 does"*). **⚠ Three limits, and the third is the one that matters.** (1) **+7 words** against B3. (2) **One of the two readers got it right**, so this is not a universal failure. (3) **We cannot show that the new wording reduces misreading.** We can observe a misreading and respond to it; **we have no way to measure whether the response works** — the same reproducibility gap `ACD-1.1-SELF-AUDIT.md` §3m records for the hand sweep. **⚠ And the converse that cuts against us hardest**: **the first two instances were ours.** If the drafting side read its own structure as a fallback twice, the text was not carrying that structure, and **the reviewers are not the ones who failed** **✅ CLOSED 2026-09-23（草案の条文で実測）**: errata E28 が草案に在る —— §4.4 は *"granted independently of Section 3, and **not as a fallback for it: it takes effect whether or not Section 3 does**"* で、**否定形から肯定形へ移っている。** |
| 170 | **The third supplied review is adversarial, names eight authorities we had never recorded, and we cannot check any of them from here.** *(The review read the 1.2 draft, and every clause number in this entry is the draft's: there §1.2 is Moral Rights, §1.4 Dedicator and §15.6 the disapplication of contra proferentem; in 1.0 they are §1.6, §1.3 and §15.3. Noted 2026-09-24.)* Where the other two found nothing, this one attacks clause by clause and cites: ***Kneschke v. LAION*** (Hamburg — whether a machine-readable TDM opt-out is effective, against §6.2/§6.3); ***Ryanair v PR Aviation*** (CJEU — Directive 96/9 Article 15 does not protect users of an **unprotected** database, so a rightsholder may restrict use by contract, against §7.2); ***Qimonda*** (cross-border insolvency defeating the 11 U.S.C. §365(n) shelter under Chapter 15, against §2.9); **17 U.S.C. §201(b)** (a contributor employed by a company does not hold what §2.6 makes them dedicate); **DMCA 17 U.S.C. §1202** (copyright management information, against §16.4's name and identifier restriction); **Japanese 消費者契約法 §8(1)** (a full exclusion of liability for intent or gross negligence is void, against §14.1); **French *droit de retrait*** (against §2.2's irrevocability); and the **EU unfair contract terms framework** (which would make §15.6's disapplication of *contra proferentem* ineffective — **the clause added today by errata E25**) | **[EXTERNAL]** **Recorded as a research queue, not as established fact, and the distinction is the whole point of this entry.** **None of these has been read at source**: this environment has no access to the reports, the judgments, or the directives, and **a case name in a supplied document is exactly the kind of claim `feedback_source_vs_content` says to weigh by content rather than by provenance** — which we cannot do until we read them. **Six of its criticisms are checkable here, and all six are misreadings that the clause itself answers**: it says §6.2/§6.3 *"unfairly nullify a third party's opt-out"*, but §6.2 states it *"does not purport to defeat a Reservation made by another rightsholder"* and §6.3 that the Dedicator *"has no power to withdraw it and this Section does not purport to give one"*; it says a plaintiff could attack §1.6 for granting to *"a subject that does not legally exist"*, but §1.6's closing words exist for that objection — the permission is granted equally to the operator and to the party on whose behalf the system acts, *"so that no permission fails for want of a legal person to hold it"*; it says a Dedicator could defeat §7.2 by forbidding scraping in a terms-of-service — but **§5.2(b) is the covenant against exactly that**, naming *"contract, terms of service, access condition, click-through, registration requirement, or any other collateral means"*; it says §2.6 makes an employed contributor dedicate what their employer owns under 17 U.S.C. §201(b), but §1.4 makes a person a Dedicator only *"to the extent that person or entity holds or may hold Covered Rights"* and §2.7 says the Dedication *"reaches only rights the Dedicator holds"*; it treats §10.1–§10.3's denial of an attribution *condition* as a trap in France and Germany, where the attribution right is inalienable — but **that right is a Moral Right under §1.2, and §12.1 waives it where waiver is possible while §12.2 covenants not to exercise it where it is not**, which is the case the objection describes; and it reads §4 as a fallback for §3 (#169). **So the same review that supplies the sharpest external material we have received also misses two in-clause qualifiers** — which is itself evidence on the readability axis (#157) rather than a reason to discount the rest. **One criticism lands on work done today**: if the unfair-terms point is right, E25's restoration of §15.6 buys less than it appeared to. **We do not withdraw E25** — its stated reason was the unordered pair of canons over §16, not the enforceability of the disapplication — **but the entry now carries the objection beside it.** **⚠ What this entry does not claim**: that the licence is defective in eight new ways. It claims that **eight named authorities are now on a list to read**, that **the list came from outside**, and that **until they are read at source, quoting them in the dossier would be borrowing an authority we have not opened**. **Seven of the eight have since been read at source** (2026-09-20 — `jurisdictions.md` §9a; the eighth, the Kneschke judgment itself, sits behind a human-verification page, though the provisions it turned on were read): **Directive 96/9/EC Article 15 says only that provisions contrary to Articles 6(1) and 8 are void**, which supports the structural point and is separately answered for us by §5.2(b); and **Directive 93/13/EEC confines its contra proferentem rule to a consumer and a seller or supplier acting in trade**, which **narrows the objection to §15.6 rather than removing it** — it bites where the Dedicator is a business, which is the adopter class B2 most lacks. **The general shape of all seven**: the outside criticism was **right in structure and too wide in scope**, and **reading the source narrowed it rather than killing it**. Two of them turned: **17 U.S.C. §201(b)'s proviso works in our favour** (a written agreement can put the rights in the contributor, and §1.4's *"to the extent"* handles both cases), and **17 U.S.C. §1202(b) prohibits removal only *"without the authority of the copyright owner"*** — §10.2 supplies that authority, so the CMI claim never arises, which makes the review's conclusion right and identical to the cost #175 already records for having no attribution requirement. **And one is favourable at source**: **UrhG 44b 条 (3) makes a TDM reservation effective only in machine-readable form**, which is precisely what §6.2 addresses by name — §6.2 answers a named provision rather than a hypothetical **潰し方**: **8 件の典拠をこの環境から読めない。** 我々にできるのは **(a) 未検証の研究キューとして分離して置き、確立した事実と混ぜないこと**（実行済み）、**(b) 取得できる経路ができた時点で 1 件ずつ原典に当てること**。**#177 はそれを 1 件やって、我々の記録の側が古いと分かった例である。** |

| 210 | **§16.4's justification finally has outside witnesses — and the same thread shows the body we are asking for approval works the opposite way.** On **2026-03-10** the OSI announced on `license-discuss` that it had *"standardized OSI license URLs using SPDX identifiers"*. ## Why this document exists

Because a submission that leads with its strengths invites the reviewer to find the weaknesses,
and the reviewer will find them. Putting them first is not humility; it is the only way the
favourable claims can be read as anything other than selection.

---

**Lost?** [`QUESTION-INDEX.md`](QUESTION-INDEX.md) indexes every worked entry in this directory by
the question it answers. [`AS-OF.md`](AS-OF.md) lists which claims about the outside world were
verified when. This file is that case.
